Greek (Kühn)

μικρὸν γὰρ ἐπ’ αὐτοῖς καὶ ταχὺ καὶ πυκνὸν 780 γίνεται τὸ πνεῦμα· μικρὸν μὲν, ὅτι διὰ τὴν στενοχωρίαν τῶν ὑποδεχομένων ἔλαττον τοῦ δέοντος ἕλκεται· πυκνὸν δὲ, ὅτι τὰ τῆς χρείας οὐ πληροῦται· διὰ δὲ τὴν αὐτὴν αἰτίαν καὶ ταχύ. δέδεικται δὲ ἐν τοῖς περὶ σφυγμῶν ὑπομνήμασιν, ὅτι τὸ μέγεθος τῆς διαστολῆς, ὅταν μὲν ἱκανῶς πληροῖ τὴν χρείαν, οὐδὲν δεῖται τάχους, ὅταν δ’ ἀπολείπηται κατά τι, τηνικαῦτα ἤδη καὶ εἰς τάχος ἡ τροπὴ γίνεται, τῆς δυνάμεως ἐῤῥωμένης, καὶ ὅταν μηδὲ τοῦθ’ ἱκανὸν ᾖ, τότε καὶ εἰς πυκνότητα. διὰ ταῦτα μὲν δὴ μικρὸν καὶ ταχὺ καὶ πυκνὸν γίνεται τὸ πνεῦμα κατὰ τοὺς σφυγμοὺς καὶ τὰς ἀναπνοὰς ἐν ταῖς προειρημέναις διαθέσεσιν, ἐῤῥέθη δ’ ἐν τοῖς περὶ σφυγμῶν, ὅτι καὶ ἀνώμαλον, καὶ τοῦτ’ ἔστι μάλιστα τὸ ἴσιον αὐτῶν γνώρισμα, καὶ ὁ τῆς αἰτίας λογισμὸς ἐν ἐκείνοις εἴρηται, καὶ ὅτι γε κατὰ μὲν αὐτὰ τὰ τὰς ἐμφράξεις ἔχοντα μόρια, καὶ ὅσα τούτοις κοινωνεῖ, τὰ τῆς ἀνωμαλίας μάλιστα εὔδηλα, καὶ ἡ μικρότης τῶν σφυγμῶν ἢ διὰ παντὸς ἢ ὡς τὸ πολὺ συνεδρεύει· κατὰ δὲ τἄλλα τὸ μέγεθος μᾶλλον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἴσιον

For in these cases the breath becomes small and quick and frequent 780: small, because on account of the narrowness of the receiving parts less than what is needed is drawn in; frequent, because the requirements of the need are not fulfilled; and for the same cause also quick. It has been shown in the treatises on pulses that the magnitude of the diastole, when it sufficiently fulfils the need, has no need at all of speed, but when it falls short in some respect, then already the change toward speed takes place, the faculty being strong, and when not even this is sufficient, then also toward frequency. For these reasons, then, the breath becomes small and quick and frequent in both pulses and respirations in the aforementioned conditions; and it was stated in the works on pulses that it also becomes irregular, and this is above all their distinctive (ἴσιον, presumably ἴδιον) sign, and the reasoning about the cause has been stated in those works, and also that in the very parts which have the obstructions, and in those which share in them, the features of the irregularity are most evident, and smallness of the pulses accompanies them either always or for the most part; whereas in the other parts it is rather magnitude.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: πνεῦμα (whether 'breath' or Galen's 'pneuma', esp. in κατὰ τοὺς σφυγμοὺς καὶ τὰς ἀναπνοὰς), ἴσιον (read as ἴδιον, 'proper'), τὸ μέγεθος μᾶλλον (sense elliptical)

For in these the breath becomes small, rapid and frequent 780: small, because, owing to the narrowness of the parts that receive it, less than is required is drawn in; frequent, because the requirements of the need are not fulfilled; and for the same cause rapid as well. It has been shown in the commentaries on pulses that the magnitude of the dilatation, when it adequately fulfils the need, requires no rapidity, but when it falls short in some respect, then the shift is made to rapidity as well, provided the faculty is strong; and when not even this is sufficient, then to frequency as well. For these reasons, then, the breath becomes small, rapid and frequent, in the pulses and in the breathing, in the conditions mentioned before. It was said in the work on pulses that it is also irregular, and that this is above all their proper sign, and the reasoning about the cause has been stated there. It was also said that in the very parts that have the obstructions, and in those that share with them, the features of irregularity are most evident, and smallness of the pulses accompanies them either always or for the most part, whereas in the other parts it is rather largeness.

K7.780-781K. VII 780–781, ch. 1.10, 1.11 difficulty high (2.65/100; thin 2, absent 1) (a) pass (b) fail 3/7 (c) judge_failed_gate unit: mixed

Greek (Kühn)

εἰ δὲ πλησίον τῆς ἀρχῆς εἴη τὸ πάθημα, κοινὸν οὕτω γίνεται 781 πάσαις ταῖς ἀρτηρίαις. ταῦτα μὲν οὖν ἐν ἐκείνοις εἴρηταί τε καὶ δέδεικται. Τὰ δὲ τῶν ἀναπνευστικῶν ὀργάνων ἴδια νῦν ἐπέξιμεν, ὡς μηδὲν ἐλλείπειν τῷ λόγῳ. ὅταν οὖν ἤτοι φυμάτων ἀπέπτων ἐν αὐτοῖς συστάντων, ἢ γλίσχρων καὶ παχέων χυμῶν καταλαβόντων τὰς ὁδοὺς τοῦ πνεύματος, ἤ τινος ὄγκου σκιῤῥώδους ἐν αὐτοῖς ὑποτραφέντος, ἢ τῶν ὁμιλούντων αὐτοῖς σωμάτων ὅτου δὴ καθ’ ὁντιναοῦν τρόπον εἰς ὄγκον ἀμετρότερον ἀρθέντος, ἔλαττον τοῦ δέοντος εἰσπνέηται διὰ στενοχωρίαν τὸ πνεῦμα, προσγίνεται ἐξ ἀνάγκης αὐτῷ διὰ τὰς εἰρημένας αἰτίας τάχος καὶ πυκνότης. ἰδέαι δέ τινες ἐν τῷ γένει τῆς δυσπνοίας ταύτης πλείους συνίστανται, ἄλλων ἄλλοτε μορίων στενοχωρουμένων ἐν τοῖς ἀναπνευστικοῖς ὀργάνοις.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

But if the affection should be near the source, it thus becomes common 781 to all the arteries. These matters, then, have been stated and demonstrated in those writings. But we shall now go through what is peculiar to the respiratory organs, so that nothing may be lacking to the account. Whenever, therefore, either because unconcocted tumours have formed in them, or because viscous and thick humours have occupied the passages of the breath, or because some scirrhous swelling has grown up within them, or because any one whatsoever of the bodies adjoining them has, in whatever manner, been raised into a swelling more immoderate than it should be, less breath than is needed is drawn in on account of the narrowness of the space, there is necessarily added to it, for the causes already stated, speed and frequency. But several forms are constituted within the genus of this dyspnoea, as now one, now another of the parts among the respiratory organs is narrowed.

Rendering B (opus-5-5)

But if the affection is near the source, it thus becomes common 781 to all the arteries. These things, then, have been both stated and demonstrated in that work. But we shall now go through what is peculiar to the respiratory organs, so that nothing may be lacking to the account. When, then, because unconcocted tumours have formed in them, or viscous and thick humours have occupied the passages of the breath, or some scirrhous swelling has grown up in them, or one of the bodies adjoining them has, in some manner whatsoever, been raised into a swelling of excessive size, the breath is drawn in to a lesser extent than is needed on account of lack of space, there necessarily accrues to it, for the reasons stated, speed and frequency. And several forms come to be within the genus of this difficulty of breathing, different parts in the respiratory organs being straitened for space at different times.

K7.781-782K. VII 781–782, ch. 1.11 difficulty high (2.84/100; thin 2, absent 3) (a) pass (b) pass 6/8 (c) judge_failed_gate unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

οἱ μὲν γὰρ κατὰ τὴν γαστέρα καὶ ἧπαρ, ἤδη δὲ καὶ τὸν σπλῆνα καὶ τὸ κῶλον καὶ τὰ τούτοις συνάπτοντα, συνιστάμενοι παρὰ φύσιν ὄγκοι στενοχωρίᾳ τοῦ διαφράγματος μικρὸν καὶ ταχὺν καὶ πυκνὸν ἐργάζονται τὸ πνεῦμα· κατὰ δὲ τὸν αὐτὸν τρόπον καὶ τοῖς ὑδερικοῖς καὶ ταῖς κυούσαις καὶ 782 τοῖς ὑπερεμπεπλησμένοις καὶ τοῖς προγάστορσι τοιοῦτον γίνεται τὸ τῆς ἀναπνοῆς εἶδος· ἐν δὲ ταῖς καλουμέναις ὀρθοπνοίαις, γλίσχρων καὶ παχέων ὑγρῶν ἐν ταῖς κατὰ τὸν πνεύμονα τραχείαις ἀρτηρίαις ἀθροιζομένων. οὕτω δὲ καὶ εἰ σκιῤῥωθείη τι κατ’ αὐτὸν, ἢ φῦμα γένοιτο, θλῖβον καὶ στενοχωροῦν τὸ τῶν τραχειῶν ἀρτηριῶν γένος, αὗται γὰρ ὑποδέχονται τὸν εἰσπνεόμενον ἀέρα, μικρὸν καὶ πυκνὸν καὶ ταχὺ ἔσται τὸ πνεῦμα. κατὰ τὸν αὐτὸν δὲ τρόπον, εἰ κᾀν τῷ θώρακι τοιοῦτον συσταίη φῦμα, οἷς δὴ πολλάκις κυφοῦνται καὶ λορδοῦνται καὶ σκολιοῦνται τὰ κατὰ τὴν ῥάχιν, μικρὸν καὶ πυκνὸν καὶ ταχὺ τὸ πνεῦμα γενήσεται, καταλαμβάνοντος δηλονότι τοῦ παρὰ φύσιν ὄγκου τὴν χώραν τοῦ πνεύμονος, εἰς ἣν διαστελλόμενον ἐξετείνετο. πολλάκις δὲ καὶ αὐτὸν ὅλον τὸν θώρακα στενώτερον αἱ τοιαῦται διαθέσεις ἀπέφηναν, ἢ κυουμέναις εὐθέως συμπεσοῦσαι, ἢ καὶ μετὰ ταῦτα ὕστερον ἀπαντήσασαι.

For the tumours contrary to nature that form in the region of the stomach and the liver, and indeed also the spleen and the colon and the parts adjoining these, by constricting the diaphragm make the breathing small and rapid and frequent; and in the same way, in dropsical patients and in pregnant women and in 782 those who have been over-filled and in those with protruding bellies, the form of respiration becomes of this kind; and so too in the so-called orthopnoeas, when viscous and thick humours collect in the rough arteries in the lung. In the same way also, if something in the lung should become scirrhous, or a phyma should arise, pressing on and constricting the class of the rough arteries—for it is these that receive the air breathed in—the breathing will be small and frequent and rapid. And in the same manner, if such a phyma should form in the thorax as well—as happens in those whose parts along the spine are often bent forward (kyphotic), bent backward (lordotic), and bent sideways (scoliotic)—the breathing will become small and frequent and rapid, the tumour contrary to nature evidently occupying the space of the lung into which it used to extend when it dilated. And often such conditions have rendered the whole thorax itself narrower, either befalling them straightaway while they are still being carried in the womb, or supervening later, after that.

For the swellings contrary to nature that arise in the stomach and liver, and also in the spleen and the colon and the parts connected with these, make the breath small, quick and frequent through the constriction of the diaphragm. In the same way, in the dropsical, in pregnant women, 782 in those who are overfilled, and in those with a protruding belly, this form of respiration comes about. In the same way, if such a growth should form in the thorax as well, of the kind by which the parts along the spine often become humped backward, bowed forward and twisted sideways, the breath will become small, frequent and quick, evidently because the swelling contrary to nature occupies the space of the lung into which it used to extend when it dilated. And often such conditions have rendered the whole thorax itself narrower, either befalling them straight away while they were being carried in the womb, or else occurring later, afterwards.

And it makes no difference, at least as regards the lung's being confined and unable to draw in sufficient air, whether the whole thorax becomes narrow in any 783 condition whatsoever, or whether it loses the intermediate space because this has been occupied by certain bodies formed in it contrary to nature, or again whether the lung itself has become scirrhous or has been obstructed or has swollen up, as happens in the affections called pneumonic and peripneumonic; for in all these cases there is this one containing cause of the inspiration's becoming small, namely the narrowing of the rough arteries; and because the inspiration is small, for this reason, as has often been said, frequency and rapidity are added to it. But when the affection befalls the lung itself, although the thorax is able to dilate to the fullest extent, they nevertheless inspire only a small amount; and so too when the spaces between the organs are occupied; but when the whole thorax, either while they are still being carried in the womb or in some other way, becomes small and narrow, it is clear that, by producing also a diastole that is small in proportion to itself, it becomes for the lung the cause of the rapidity in inspiration; and so too when the diaphragm is compressed by something, specifically at the lower boundary itself of the thorax, as in the painless affections.

It makes no difference, at least as regards the lung's being unable, when constricted, to draw in a sufficient quantity of air, whether the whole thorax has become narrow through some condition or other, 783 or the lung has lost the intervening space because it has been occupied by certain bodies formed contrary to nature within the thorax, or whether the lung itself has become scirrhous or obstructed or swollen, as happens in the so-called pneumonic and peripneumonic affections. So too when the diaphragm is compressed by something, specifically at the lower boundary itself of the thorax, as in the painless affections.

Let us therefore no longer make mention in this discussion of those suffering from pleuritis, or of those who have certain abscesses or, in general, painful swellings in the thorax; for the whole preceding discussion was being carried through concerning the painless affections, all those which, by compressing the lung and narrowing the rough arteries within it, make the inspiration smaller. Rather, let us assign those to the affections of the thorax described before these, in which we said that the thorax, being less distended on account of the pain, makes the breath small; but the condition of those called empyics, even if its origin very often arises from pleuritis, let us count together with those now mentioned, which, producing narrowness 785 in the lung without pain, render the respiration small. For indeed the pus contained in it in such conditions becomes the cause of the smallness in the distension. Accordingly, since it is itself no longer dilated in accordance with nature, neither does it make the drawing-in of the breath equal. In what way, then, will these affections of the respiratory organs that are without pain—for this still remains for the present discussion—be distinguished from those with pain, if indeed the breath becomes small and frequent alike in both? Or is it by the speed of the movement? For in those with pain they breathe little and frequently, yet not quickly, at least whenever such an affection occurs without fever; with fever, however, even if they breathe similarly in smallness and in speed, nevertheless they are distinguished by the frequency.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For in them the breath becomes frequent to no moderate degree; but this does not hold for those who are dyspnoeic because of narrowness of the respiratory organs: rather, even if their breath should become more frequent, it undergoes this moderately and far below the extreme 786 of frequency. But also the expiration in those who have fever is at once both greater and hotter, which was not the case for those who, because of narrowness, breathe out small and frequent. And how one must accurately diagnose the smallness and the greatness of each of the two parts of the whole respiration will be stated a little later, once we have first completed the whole account concerning the organs. For that their dyspnoeas without pain differ from those with pain has indeed been stated. But whether, just as we declared without qualification that the narrowness must extend into the lung, and especially into its rough arteries, in order for the animal to become dyspnoeic, so too it must be said that the pain must arise in the respiratory organs, and especially in the thorax—for the lung is very nearly insensible—or whether we must seek some other pronouncement in the case of such dyspnoeas?

For neither, I think, would the man who says that this kind of dyspnoea follows only upon the pains of the respiratory organs seem to have judged well about them as a whole, nor to have remembered the earlier 787 reasonings, in which it was shown that the breath becomes small and frequent not on account of the affection, but on account of the faculty of the activity, which is psychic. The kind of dyspnoea, however, that follows upon narrowings of the lung needs nothing further from the faculty for its generation; hence in that case we pronounced rightly, making our statement about the matter universally. But concerning the painful affections one ought not to have spoken without qualification of their arising in the respiratory parts. For it was not on account of the nature of the parts themselves specifically, but because a psychic faculty governed their motion, that the respiration came to turn toward smallness, so that the animal might not be pained more greatly, the faculty separating (expanding) the thorax less than is proper. Is it, then, only in this respect that the pulses stand dissimilar to the respirations, or is it possible to find some difference also in respect of the 789 cause deriving from the faculty itself, taken on its own, apart from its being interwoven with the organs? our mind; but whenever we cease and no longer have our thought on anything except walking alone, then, as if roused from some sleep, we both recognise the deficiency of the present activity relative to the original impulse and again rouse ourselves to the task—so too those who are delirious (παραπαίοντες), since, I think, they have their mind in their phantasms, as if in certain preoccupations, neglect the psychic activities, and neither urinate, unless someone reminds them, nor defecate, nor ask to drink or to eat, nor any other such thing; and if they should happen to ask at some time, in the interval, while the thing is being brought, they have forgotten, and again they need reminders. And just as all these perform their activities at long intervals, so too they perform them large: at any rate they urinate a very great quantity all at once, and drink at a single draught beyond the due measure, and do each of the other things likewise. And there is nothing, I think, remarkable in this; for what they ought to have done many times at the appropriate times, they are compelled to fulfil all at once when the need presses, and especially whenever it is necessary for life.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For someone who drinks less than is needed is capable of holding out not 791 only for days but even for whole months, whereas someone who breathes less would not last even for the smallest part of the day. Hence, even if those who are delirious drink a very great amount at a single time, nevertheless, because they take it only at long intervals, their total drink falls far short of the due measure; but the need of respiration, when it is not fulfilled, because it involves a danger that is at once imminent and distressing, both rouses them to the activity and does not allow slackness even for a very short time, but immediately recovers all of what it has been deprived of through the time of rest, and thus it becomes not only rare but also great, and greater than is proper by just as much as it is rarer—which does not happen in the case of drink; for they drink much more rarely but only a little more, whence Hippocrates called them 'small drinkers' (brachypotai). This argument, then, demonstrates that the respiration of those who are delirious turns to rarity and greatness; and direct observation too bears witness not least, at any rate for those who are willing to test the theory on the very works of the art.

For someone who drinks less than is needed is able to hold out not 791 for days only, but even for whole months, whereas someone who breathes less could not last even for the smallest part of a day. Hence, even if the delirious drink a very great amount at one time, yet, because it is taken at long intervals, the whole of their drink falls far short of the moderate amount; but the need of respiration, when it is not fulfilled, because it carries a danger at once imminent and distressing, rouses them to the activity and does not allow them to be slack even for a very short time, and at once recovers all that it has been deprived of through the time of rest; and thus [the breath] becomes not only infrequent but also large, and larger than is proper by as much as it is also more infrequent, which does not happen in the case of drink: for they drink much more infrequently, but only a little more, whence Hippocrates called them 'drinkers of little' (brachypotai). This argument, then, demonstrates that in the delirious the breathing turns to infrequency and largeness; and not least does direct observation also bear witness to it, at least for those who wish to examine the theory upon the very works of the art.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

But as many as, in their lecture-sessions with the young, seated upon high thrones, lofty 792 and conspicuous, have neglected the works of the art and babble whatever comes to their tongue—for these men the things attested by what is clearly apparent are no more credible than for those who do not even know in the first place whether such-and-such a thing appears at all. Indeed, one of such sophists once disputed with me about the breath's becoming large and rare in those who are delirious. For he supposed respiration to be one of the vital functions, but the phenomenon did not agree with such a supposition, whence he decided that he must come to close quarters with it. For he said that such a form of respiration does not appear at all in states of delirium. For the things which the sophists have never once attempted to observe upon the sick themselves, about these they shamelessly insist as though they had seen them ten thousand times, and they do not hesitate to profess that they have long experience of things never seen by them even in a dream. But those at least who observe the phenomena and the things that befall the sick write just as Hippocrates also does.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For when the organs are in their natural state, the quantity of the pneuma is always proportionate to the dilations 794 of these; not, however, when they are disposed contrary to nature, but in that case it is possible at times for the thorax to be distended to the greatest extent while only a little of the external air is inspired. For there are many conditions, if we remember at all what was said before, by which the lung, being constricted, is unable to receive sufficient air; therefore the inspirations come about in proportion not to the conditions of all the respiratory organs, but only to those of the rough arteries. But the dilation of these cannot be discerned by any single one of the senses; therefore one should not even direct one's attention to the respiratory organs whenever we wish to discern the quantity of the inspiration. For it suffices to examine only the air itself, how much is inspired and how much in turn is expired. For neither do we infer anything about the amount of food from the bulk of the belly, but here too it suffices to examine only the quantity of what is eaten.

ἐπεὶ δ’ ἐν ταῖς ἐκπνοαῖς ποτὲ μὲν ἀργοῦσι παντελῶς οἱ μύες, ἐπιτρέποντες τῷ θώρακι καταπίπτοντι τὴν οἰκείαν ἀπολαμβάνειν κατάστασιν, ποτὲ δ’ ἐνεργοῦντες ἐπὶ πλέον 795 αὐ τὸν συστέλλουσιν, ἀπεδείχθη γὰρ ἡμῖν πολλάκις περὶ τούτων, αἱ μὲν χωρὶς τῆς τούτων συντονίας συστολαὶ τοῦ θώρακος οὐκ ἄν ποτε μεγάλην ἐκπνοὴν ἐργάσαιντο, αἱ δὲ μετὰ ταύτης, αἵπερ δὴ καὶ ἐκφυσήσεις εἰσὶ, ὡς καὶ τοῦτ’ ἐδείχθη, δημιουργοὶ τοῦ μεγέθους εἰσίν. ἀλλ’ οὐδ’ ἐπὶ τούτων γυμνοῦντας τὸν ἄνθρωπον ὁρᾷν ἀξιῶ τὴν τάσιν τῶν μυῶν, ἀλλ’ ἀρκεῖ τὸ μετ’ ἐκφυσήσεως εἶδος τῆς ἐκπνοῆς γνώρισμα τίθεσθαι μέγιστον. ἐπειγούσης γοῦν τῆς χρείας ἀθρόον ἐκπνεῖν ἀναγκαζόμεθα, ὥσπερ καὶ ὅταν ἡ τῆς εἰσπνοῆς ἐπείγῃ χρεία, πολὺν καὶ ἀθρόον ἕλκομεν τὸν ἔξωθεν ἀέρα. Ἐπειδὴ δὲ καὶ περὶ τούτων αὐτάρκως διήλθομεν, τὰ κεφάλαια τῶν ἀπ’ ἀρχῆς εἰρημένων ἀναλαβόντες, ἐπὶ τὰ λείποντα τραπώμεθα.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: συντονίας, ἐκφυσήσεις, ἐκφυσήσεως

But since in expirations the muscles are sometimes wholly inactive, allowing the thorax, as it falls back, to regain its proper condition, and sometimes, acting, 795 contract it still further—for this has often been demonstrated by us—the contractions of the thorax without the intense exertion of these muscles could never produce a large expiration, whereas those accompanied by it, which are indeed also blowings-out (ἐκφυσήσεις), as this too was shown, are the producers of largeness. But not even in these cases do I think it right to strip the person and look at the tension of the muscles; rather it suffices to set down the form of expiration accompanied by blowing-out as the greatest mark. At any rate, when the need is pressing we are compelled to expire all at once, just as also, when the need for inspiration is pressing, we draw in the outside air in large quantity and all at once. And since we have gone through these matters too sufficiently, let us take up again the chief points of what has been said from the beginning and turn to what remains.

Since in expirations the muscles are sometimes entirely idle, allowing the thorax, as it collapses, to regain its proper state, and sometimes, being active, contract it further 795 (for this has been demonstrated by us many times), contractions of the thorax without the intensity of these muscles would never produce a large expiration, whereas those accompanied by it, which are in fact also blowings-out, as this too was shown, are the producers of largeness. But not even in these cases do I think it right to strip the person and look at the tension of the muscles; it is enough to take the form of expiration accompanied by blowing out as the most important sign. At any rate, when the need presses, we are compelled to breathe out all at once, just as also, when the need for inspiration presses, we draw in the outside air in large quantity and all at once. And since we have now gone through these matters sufficiently as well, let us recapitulate the chief points of what has been said from the beginning and then turn to what remains.

The chief points were, as I think, these: having discovered the simple dyspnoeas, to connect them with the containing causes of respiration, and to show the efficient cause of each of them, and again, for each of the causes, the dyspnoea that follows upon it—in the course of which we were in fact compelled 796 to make mention also of composite dyspnoeas. But in what respect the matters concerning the pulses agree with the respirations, and in what respect they also differ—this too was no small chief point among those we went through. Since all these have been brought to completion, let us next examine the composite dyspnoeas, for these still remain, at least for the first of the commentaries we proposed. And once the simple ones have been discovered, it is no longer at all difficult to discover these too, compounded as they are out of those. For since there is a certain large inspiration, and likewise also a swift one, and the opposites of these, a small and a slow one, it is necessary that the composite dyspnoeas concerning inspiration be four in all: the large and at the same time slow, the large and swift, the small and swift, and fourth the small and slow. So too in the case of expiration the same four conjugations must exist, two of them large and two small, divided by swiftness and slowness. In the rests, however, it is not possible for a composite form of dyspnoea to arise.

The chief points were, I think, these: having discovered the simple dyspnoeas, to connect them with the sustaining causes of respiration, and to show for each of them the cause that produces it, and again, for each of the causes, the dyspnoea that follows upon it; and in doing this we were compelled also to 796 mention compound dyspnoeas. Once the simple ones have been discovered, it is no longer at all difficult to discover these as well, since they are compounded from those. For since there is a large inspiration, and likewise a quick one, and their opposites, the small and the slow, there must necessarily be four compound dyspnoeas in all with respect to inspiration: the large and at the same time slow, the large and quick, the small and quick, and fourth the small and slow. In the same way, with respect to expiration too, the same four combinations must necessarily exist: two large and two small, divided by quickness and slowness. In the pauses, however, it is not possible for a compound form of dyspnoea to arise.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

797 For they did not have two oppositions, as the movements do, but only one, constituted according to density and rarity. And indeed, when the movements themselves are compared with one another—I mean inspiration with expiration, just as in the pulses diastole with systole—there will be certain forms of rhythms of the whole respiration. For it is possible for the inspiration to be large and the expiration small; or conversely the inspiration small and the expiration large; or both small, or both large. So too both swift, or both slow, or the one swift and the other slow. And indeed there is also a certain uneven respiration, just as there is an uneven pulse, both that which is in a collection of movements and that which is in a single movement; and likewise respiration would become ordered and disordered, just as the pulse does; and as many as are the differences of the uneven pulses, both those in a single movement and those constituted in several, so many also are those of respirations.

Rendering B (opus-5-5)

797 For they did not have two antitheses, as the movements did, but only one, consisting in frequency and infrequency. Moreover, when the movements themselves are compared with one another (I mean inspiration with expiration, just as in the pulses diastole with systole), there will be certain forms of rhythm of the whole respiration. For it is possible for the inspiration to be large and the expiration small; or conversely the inspiration small and the expiration large; or both small, or both large. So too both may be quick, or both slow, or the one quick and the other slow. Moreover, there is also an uneven respiration, just as there is an uneven pulse, both that in a collection of movements and that within a single movement. As many as are the differences of uneven pulses, both of those within a single movement and of those consisting in several, so many are there also of respirations.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

798 But, as I said right at the beginning of the discourse, all of them have treated of respiration altogether carelessly, and I may state the cause of their negligence concerning it as the discussion proceeds. For now, we must bring to completion what lies before us, beginning from the irregularity in a single movement, since this would seem by its very nature to be ranked between those that are strictly simple and those that are admittedly composite. At any rate, someone once disputed with us about it, maintaining that it is not composite—something that would not have happened to him had it not been close to the simple ones. But that it is composite is clear from the following: if someone, when beginning to inspire, draws in the air quickly, but at the end slowly, or conversely passes over from a slow beginning to a quick end, we would say that this inspiration is neither quick nor slow simply, but evidently composite, having one part of itself swift and another slow.

But that it is compound is clear from the following. If someone, on beginning to breathe in, draws in the air quickly but at the end slowly, or conversely passes from a slow beginning to a quick end, we would not say that this inspiration is simply either quick or slow, but evidently compound, having one part of itself swift and another slow.

Greek (Kühn)

κατὰ ταὐτὸν δὲ καὶ τὴν ἐκπνοὴν, εἰ παντοίως ἀλλοιοῦται, μεταπιπτούσης τῆς κινήσεως ἢ ἐκ βραδυτῆτος εἰς τάχος, ἢ ἔμπαλιν ἐκ τάχους εἰς 799 βραδυτῆτα, συγκεῖσθαι φήσομεν ἐξ ἐναντίως ἐχόντων πρὸς ἄλληλα τῶν μορίων, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο μηδ’ ἁπλῆν ἀλλὰ σύνθετον ὑπάρχειν. ὡσαύτως δὲ καὶ ὅταν ἡσυχία τις σχίζῃ τε καὶ διακόπτῃ τὴν ἑτέραν τῶν κινήσεων, ἤτοι τὴν εἰσπνοὴν ἢ τὴν ἐκπνοὴν, σύνθετον καὶ τότε φήσομεν εἶναι τὴν διῃρημένην, ἥ τις ἂν εἴη. ὦπται δέ ποτε σπανίως οὐ θάτερον μόνον, ἀλλ’ ἀμφότερα τὰ μόρια τῆς ὅλης ἀναπνοῆς κατὰ τὸν αὐτὸν ἄνθρωπον οὕτως ἔχοντα, καὶ γίγνεσθαι μάλιστα τὸ τοιοῦτον, ὅταν ὑγρῶν πλῆθος ἠθροισμένον ἐν ταῖς τραχείαις ἀρτηρίαις ἀποπνίγῃ τὸν ἄνθρωπον, ἐκ πλευρίτιδος ὀλεθρίας ἢ περιπνευμονίας μὴ ἐκκαθαρθείσης ἢ ἐμπυήματος αὐτόθι συστάντος, ὥστε κατὰ τοῦτο τὰς ἀναπνοὰς ὁμολογεῖν τοῖς σφυγμοῖς. ἐδείκνυτο γὰρ οὖν καὶ ἐπ’ ἐκείνων ἐμφράξεσιν ὀργάνων καὶ θλίψεσι καὶ ὅλως στενοχωρίαις ἡ ἀνωμαλία γιγνομένη. καὶ τοίνυν ὅσα περὶ τῶν ἄλλων ἀρτηριῶν τῶν καθ’ ὅλον τὸ ζῶον ἐν τῷ δευτέρῳ περὶ τῶν ἐν σφυγμοῖς αἰτίων εἴρηται μαθών τις εὐπορώτερος ἂν εἴη περὶ τῶν ἐν πνεύμονι τραχειῶν καλουμένων λέγειν τὰ παραπλήσια.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

In the same way we shall also say that the expiration, if it is altered in any manner whatever, the movement shifting either from slowness to speed or, conversely, from speed to 799 slowness, is composed of parts standing in opposition to one another, and for this reason is not simple but composite. Likewise too, when some pause splits and interrupts one of the two movements, either the inspiration or the expiration, then too we shall say that the one divided, whichever it may be, is composite. And it has sometimes, though rarely, been seen that not only one or the other but both parts of the whole respiration are in this condition in the same person, and that such a thing occurs especially when a quantity of humours accumulated in the rough arteries suffocates the person, as a result of a deadly pleuritis or a peripneumonia that has not been cleared out, or an empyema that has formed in that place, so that in this respect respirations agree with the pulses. For it was shown in their case too that the unevenness arises through obstructions of the organs and compressions and, in general, narrowings. And accordingly, someone who has learned what has been said about the other arteries, those throughout the whole animal, in the second book On the Causes in the Pulses, would be better equipped to say similar things about the so-called rough arteries in the lung.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: παντοίως, ἐκκαθαρθείσης

In the same way also the expiration, if it is altered in every way (the motion changing either from slowness to speed or, conversely, from speed to 799 slowness), we shall say is composed of parts that stand in a contrary relation to one another, and for this reason is not simple but compound. Likewise also, when some rest splits and cuts through one of the two motions, either the inspiration or the expiration, we shall say that then too the divided one, whichever it may be, is compound. And it has sometimes, though rarely, been seen that not only one of the two, but both parts of the whole respiration are so disposed in the same person; and that such a thing occurs especially when a quantity of fluids collected in the rough arteries is choking the person, as a result of a deadly pleuritis, or of a peripneumonia that has not been cleared out, or of an empyema formed there, so that in this respect the respirations agree with the pulses. For in the case of those too the unevenness was shown to arise from obstructions of the organs and compressions and, in general, narrowings. And so anyone who has learned what was said about the other arteries, those throughout the whole animal, in the second book On the Causes in Pulses would be better equipped to say similar things about the so-called rough [arteries] in the lung.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For the former 800 must be narrowed in order for the pulse to become uneven, but the rough arteries in order for the respiration to become so. And it was said in those books that unevenness of the pulses could also arise through weakness of the faculty, when it is unable to dilate the arteries completely at a single stroke. For they stop midway and take a rest, just as old men do on their journeys. And at times a certain unevenness could arise also in respiration, both that within a single activity alone and that which is called systematic, through some weakness of the faculty that produces it. But since this faculty is psychic, whereas the one that produces the pulses is vital, there would be a certain difference with respect to the prognoses drawn from them: the pulses always indicate the weakness of the faculty in proportion to the damage in themselves, whereas the respirations no longer do so in the same way, unless one additionally takes into account the choice of the patient.

Greek (Kühn)

εἰ γὰρ ἐκ κοσμίου καὶ τὴν ἀπόκρισιν τὴν θρασεῖαν κακὸν εἶναί φησιν ὁ Ἱπποκράτης, οὐ μέντοι ἐπὶ τῶν θρασέων φύσει μὴ ὅτι τὴν τοιαύτην ἀπόκρισιν, ἀλλὰ μηδ’ αὐτὴν τὴν παραφροσύνην μέγα τι 801 δηλοῦν νόμιζε. οὕτως ἔχει καὶ ἐπὶ τῆς ἀναπνοῆς. οἱ μὲν γὰρ ἀταλαίπωροι καὶ μαλακοὶ καθ’ ἅπαν ἔργον ἀνωμάλως ἀναπνέουσι, κᾂν μηδὲν αὐτοὺς μέγα βιάζοιτο· ὅσοι δὲ φιλόπονοι καὶ γενναῖοι τὴν γνώμην, οὐκ ἄν ποτε, πρὶν εἰς ἐσχάτην ἀῤῥωστίαν ἀφικέσθαι τὴν δύναμιν, ἀνωμάλως ἀναπνεύσειαν. ἐπὶ δὲ τῆς κατὰ τοὺς σφυγμοὺς ἀνωμαλίας ἡ αὐτὴ πρόγνωσις τῆς διαθέσεως ἔν τε τοῖς βελτίοσι τὴν γνώμην καὶ τοῖς χείροσι. καθόλου γὰρ εἰπεῖν, ὅλως ὅπου μηδὲν συντελεῖ πρὸς τὴν ἐνέργειαν ἡ προαίρεσις, ἐνταῦθα τὸ δηλούμενον ἴσον ἅπασιν· ἐν οἷς δ’ ἤτοι τὸ πᾶν ἢ τὸ πλεῖστον, ἐπὶ τούτοις προσεπισκοπεῖσθαι χρὴ τὸ ἦθος τοῦ κάμνοντος. ὥσπερ δὲ τῶν δημιουργουσῶν τὰς ἐνεργείας δυνάμεων διαφορὰ κατά τε τοὺς σφυγμοὺς καὶ τὰς ἀναπνοὰς διαφόρους ἐργάζεται τὰς προγνώσεις, οὕτω καὶ παρ’ αὐτὴν τὴν τῶν ὀργάνων φύσιν ἔσται τις ἐνδείξεως ἑτερότης. οἱ μὲν γὰρ σφυγμοὶ διὰ τῶν λείων ἀρτηριῶν, ἀναπνοαὶ δὲ οὐ διὰ μόνων γίγνονται τῶν τραχειῶν ἀρτηριῶν, ἀλλ’ οὐχ ἧττον αὐτῶν, εἰ μὴ ἄρα καὶ μᾶλλον διὰ τοῦ μυώδους γένους.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For if Hippocrates says that even a bold answer from an orderly man is a bad thing, yet in the case of those who are bold by nature do not suppose that—let alone such an answer—even derangement itself signifies anything great. 801 So it is also in the case of respiration. For those who are unenduring of hardship and soft in every task breathe unevenly, even if nothing great constrains them; whereas those who are industrious and noble in disposition would never breathe unevenly before the faculty has come to extreme weakness. But in the case of the unevenness in the pulses the prognosis of the condition is the same both in those who are better in disposition and in those who are worse. For, to speak generally, wherever volition contributes nothing at all to the activity, there what is signified is equal for all; but in those cases where it contributes either everything or the greater part, in these one must additionally examine the character of the patient. And just as the difference of the faculties that produce the activities makes the prognoses different in the case of pulses and respirations, so too, owing to the very nature of the organs, there will be a certain diversity of indication. For the pulses come about through the smooth arteries, but respirations come about not through the rough arteries alone, but no less than through them—if not indeed even more—through the muscular kind.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐκ κοσμίου, μὴ ὅτι ... ἀλλὰ μηδ’, ἐν οἷς δ’ ἤτοι τὸ πᾶν ἢ τὸ πλεῖστον

For if Hippocrates says that from an orderly person even a bold reply is a bad sign, then in those who are bold by nature you should consider that not only such a reply, but not even delirium itself, signifies anything 801 of great moment. Rendering A (fable-5-1)

802 For this is what the psychic faculty first moves for the generation of respiration; and the thorax, moved as a whole by this, in its turn moves the lung along with itself, in the manner we demonstrated in our reasonings about these matters. The rough arteries are a part of the lung, and into these the air that is breathed in first comes. Just as, then, the affections of these often render the whole respiration uneven, so too do those of the muscles—the former because they are the spaces for the pneuma, the muscles because they are the primary organ of the motion. But it is not the same affection in each of the two kinds of organs that renders the respiration uneven, since the features of their use are not similar either. If, then, each of the organs, when diseased, harms the activity in those very respects in which, when healthy, it benefits it, the damage of the rough arteries is a certain narrowness, while that of the muscles is a faulty and disorderly motion; for the damage of the former is as of spaces for the pneuma, that of the latter as of organs of motion.

Greek (Kühn)

τῆς μὲν οὖν ἐν ταῖς τραχείαις στενοχωρίας τοὺς παρὰ φύσιν ὄγκους ᾐτιασάμην, τῶν δ’ ἐν τοῖς μυσὶ ﻿ 803 πλημμελῶν κινήσεων τὰς προφάσεις νῦν ἐροῦμεν. ἔστιν οὖν ἴδια παθήματα μυῶν σπασμοὶ καὶ παλμοὶ καὶ τρόμοι, ὡς δι’ ἑτέρων ἀποδέδεικται· ἀλλὰ καὶ ὅτι πλημμελεῖς κινήσεις εἰσὶν, οὐ δεῖται λόγου. εἴπερ δ’ ἄμφω ταῦθ’ ὑπάρχοι τοῖς παθήμασιν, ὥστε καὶ πλημμελέσι κινήσεσιν εἶναι καὶ μυῶν, αὐτὰ δὲ ταῦτ’ εἴη τὰ ζητούμενα, ἤδη πέρας ἂν ἡμῖν ἔχοι καὶ ὅδε ὁ λόγος· ἔσται γάρ ποτ’ ἀνώμαλος ἡ ἀναπνοὴ καὶ διά τι τούτων. ὡς γὰρ καὶ ἡ βάδισις ἀνώμαλος γίνεται τῶν ἐν τοῖς σκέλεσι μυῶν ἤτοι τρεμόντων ἢ παλλομένων ἢ σπωμένων, οὕτω καὶ ἀναπνοὴ γίνοιτ’ ἄν ποτε ἀνώμαλος διὰ τοὺς κινοῦντας τὸν θώρακα μῦς, ὡς εἴρηται, βεβλαμμένους. ὥστε καὶ πασῶν τῶν ἀνωμαλιῶν, τῶν τε κατὰ μίαν ἐνέργειαν καὶ τῶν κατὰ πολλὰς ἑξῆς συνισταμένων, ἤτοι στενοχωρία τῶν τραχειῶν ἀρτηριῶν, ἢ τῶν τοῦ θώρακος μυῶν πλημμελὴς κίνησις, ἢ ἀῤῥωστία τῆς δημιουργούσης αὐτὴν δυνάμεως αἴτιον γενήσεται.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

Of the narrowing in the rough [arteries], then, I have assigned the swellings contrary to nature as cause; of the faulty movements in the muscles 803 we shall now state the occasions. Now the affections proper to muscles are spasms and palpitations and tremors, as has been demonstrated elsewhere; and that these are faulty movements needs no argument. And if both these things belong to the affections, so that they are both faulty movements and movements of muscles, and these very things are what is being sought, then this argument too would already have its end for us; for respiration will at some time be uneven also through one of these. For just as walking too becomes uneven when the muscles in the legs are either trembling or palpitating or in spasm, so too respiration would at some time become uneven because the muscles that move the thorax have been damaged, as has been said. So that of all the unevennesses, both those which arise within a single activity and those which arise over many in succession, the cause will be either narrowing of the rough arteries, or faulty movement of the muscles of the thorax, or weakness of the faculty that produces it.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: προφάσεις, πλημμελῶν, τῶν κατὰ πολλὰς ἑξῆς συνισταμένων, αὐτὴν

Now, for the constriction in the rough (arteries) I blamed the swellings contrary to nature; of the faulty movements in the muscles 803 we shall now state the causes. Spasms, palpitations and tremors, then, are affections peculiar to muscles, as has been demonstrated in other works; and that they are faulty movements needs no argument. If both of these belong to these affections, so that they are both faulty movements and movements of muscles, and if these are the very things being sought, then this discussion too would already have reached its end; for breathing will sometimes be irregular through one of these as well. For just as walking becomes irregular when the muscles in the legs either tremble or palpitate or are in spasm, so breathing too might sometimes become irregular because the muscles that move the thorax are, as has been said, impaired. So for all the irregularities, both those within a single activity and those that arise across many successive activities, the cause will be either constriction of the rough arteries, or faulty movement of the muscles of the thorax, or weakness of the faculty that produces it.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

It has been written, as I said, at very great length about 804 all the irregularities in the second book of the Causes in the Pulses, and for this reason we pass over everything that is common to that discussion, merely reminding the reader of it; but whatever is peculiar to respiration we shall go through in this treatise. Now the matters pertaining to the use are, as was also said before, common, and those pertaining to the rough arteries are analogous to those of the smooth ones; but what belongs to the respirations as peculiar and exceptional in comparison with the pulses is the class of the muscles. The faculties that produce them stand in one respect analogously, but in another respect not so. For insofar as they are active causes, in this respect they stand in analogy; but insofar as choice (προαίρεσις) governs the respiratory faculty, whereas of the vital faculty there is no higher master, but it is completely free, in this respect they differ. And therefore respiration will have something additional, over and above the pulses, toward the generation of irregularity, which also let us not pass over.

And it is twofold: the one proceeding from the psychic faculty itself alone, the other from its entanglement with pains; that from the 805 faculty alone is slight and hard to recognise, while that due to the pains is greater, clearer and more varied. For in deliriums rarity and largeness predominate, but a certain irregularity and disorder also appears, both over several respirations and sometimes in a single one alone, inasmuch as, I suppose, the phantasms too at one time more, at another less, displace the reasoning from its natural state. For in such imaginings it is reasonable that the respiration too should change somewhat and become varied, towards the generation of irregularity and disorder, and contrary to the quantity of the need. For so long as each of the activities falls short of it by only a little, the alteration from the natural state is small; but when a sufficiently large deficiency relative to the natural state arises, then it is now necessary that the quantity of the rests, and of the movements not only the quantity but now also the quality, be altered, and thereby that the whole respiration become irregular and disordered. And this same thing must necessarily happen in the case of pains too.

For not even in their case is the need fulfilled in each activity; for they are able to dilate the thorax neither quickly nor frequently nor to the greatest extent; for all these things are 806 distressing to them, most of all the magnitude, second the speed, and then frequency as well; for it is less painful for those in pain to be moved over a longer time than over a shorter one. For this reason, then, they take away a very great deal from the magnitude, very little from the speed, while from the frequency they take away nothing, but even add something, correcting the deficiency relative to the natural [respirations] which arises from the smallness. The addition, however, is not so great as to make up all that is lacking. Therefore whatever portion of the need is left unfulfilled, accumulating over many successive activities, when the animal has already come near to being suffocated, then compels the choice too, for some time at least, to make the respiration greater and much quicker and more frequent than before.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

But in the activities of this [breathing], while the need is being fulfilled, yet the animal is being worn down by the pains, the will again subtracts both from the magnitude and from the speed and from the frequency, at one time more from one, at another from another, inasmuch as it is at a loss 807 and is able neither to halt the activity, for fear of suffocation, nor to find any cure for the pain. At any rate it passes now to one thing, now to another, the one as something that will fulfil the need, the other as something that will soothe the pain. But since, of all these, those that fulfil the need exacerbate the pains, while those that relieve the pains increase the need, and no common remedy for both is found, it passes in manifold ways to everything out of perplexity, always being dissatisfied with the present one. And from this arises the unevenness, both in each breath singly and in all the breaths in succession, and the manifold disorder. For in the destruction of equality lies the genesis of all unevennesses and disorders. For this too was shown in the books On the Differences of Pulses, that equality in succession is evenness, while equality according to periods is order; and that when the equality in succession is destroyed, unevenness arises, and when that according to periods is destroyed, disorder.

For this too was shown in the work on the differences of pulses: that consecutive equality is regularity, while periodic equality is order; and that when consecutive equality is destroyed, irregularity arises, and when periodic equality is destroyed, disorder.

ἀλλ’ ὅμως πολλῆς μὲν ἀνωμαλίας, πολλῆς δὲ ἀταξίας ταῖς τοιαύταις διαθέσεσι ﻿ 808 συμπιπτούσης, ἐπικρατεῖ τὸ κατὰ μικρότητα καὶ πυκνότητα γένος τῆς δυσπνοίας. εἰ γὰρ καὶ μέγα ποτ’ ἀπαπνεύσειαν εἰς ἅπαξ, οἷον ἀποπειρώμενοι τῆς τοιαύτης ἀναπνοῆς, ἀφίστανται παραχρῆμα διὰ τὴν ὀδύνην, καὶ χρόνῳ γε παμπόλλῳ μικρὸν πάνυ ἀναπνέουσιν, ἄχρι περ ἂν ὑπὸ τῆς χρείας ἐπειγούσης ἀναγκασθέντες ἐφ’ ἑτέραν αὖθις πεῖραν τράπωνται μεγάλης ἀναπνοῆς. τὸ συνεχὲς οὖν τῆς ἀνωμαλίας καὶ ἀτάκτου μεταβολῆς αὐτοῖς ἐν τάχει καὶ βραδυτῆτι καὶ ταῖς τῆς πυκνότητος ἐξαλλαγαῖς μάλιστα συνίσταται, μηδέποτε μὲν ἁπλῶς ἀραιᾶς γιγνομένης τῆς ἀναπνοῆς διὰ τὴν τῆς χρείας ἔπειξιν, ἧττον μέντοι καὶ μᾶλλον πυκνῆς, κᾀν τούτῳ τὴν ἀνωμαλίαν κτωμένης. ὥστ’ ἔστι πυκνὸν μὲν ἀεὶ τοῖς οὕτω διακειμένοις τὸ πνεῦμα, μέγα δὲ σπανίως, βραδὺ δ’ οὐδὲν μᾶλλον ἢ ταχὺ, διὰ τὴν εἰς ἑκάτερον αὐτῶν ὀξύῤῥοπον μετάπτωσιν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀπαπνεύσειαν

But nevertheless, although much irregularity and much disorder befall such conditions, 808 the kind of dyspnoea characterised by smallness and frequency prevails. For even if at some time they should breathe largely once (ἀπαπνεύσειαν), as though making trial of such a breath, they desist at once on account of the pain, and for a very long time they breathe quite small, until, compelled by the urgent need, they turn once again to another attempt at a large breath. The continuity, then, of the irregularity and of the disordered change in them consists chiefly in speed and slowness and in the alterations of frequency, the respiration never becoming simply rare, on account of the urgency of the need, yet being less and more frequent, and in this acquiring its irregularity. So that in those who are so disposed the breath is always frequent, large rarely, and slow no more than quick, on account of the abrupt shift toward either of them.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀπαπνεύσειαν, ὀξύῤῥοπον

Nevertheless, although much unevenness and much disorder occur in such conditions 808, the class of difficulty of breathing that consists in smallness and frequency prevails. For even if at some point they should take a large breath (reading ἀπαπνεύσειαν as 'breathe') all at once, as if making trial of such breathing, they immediately desist because of the pain, and for a very long time they breathe very small, until, compelled by the pressing need, they turn once more to another attempt at a large breath. The continuity of their unevenness and disordered change, then, consists chiefly in speed and slowness and in the variations of frequency. Because of the urgency of the need, the breathing never becomes simply infrequent; it becomes more and less frequent, and in this it acquires its unevenness. So in those who are so disposed the breath is always frequent, rarely large, and no more slow than fast, because of its quick-tipping transition into either of these.

Greek (Kühn)

ὀρθῶς οὖν ἔμπροσθεν ἐῤῥέθη πᾶσι τοῖς ὀργάνοις ὀδυνωμένοις, ὅσα κινεῖται κατὰ τὰς ἀναπνοὰς, μικρὸν καὶ πυκνὸν ἕπεσθαι τὸ πνεῦμα· κατὰ δὲ τὸν αὐτὸν λόγον ἐν ταῖς παραφροσύναις μέγα τε γίγνεσθαι καὶ ἀραιὸν, εἰ καὶ ὅτι μάλιστα 809 καὶ ταύταις ἀνωμαλία τις ἕπεται. πλεονεκτεῖ γὰρ ἐν αὐταῖς ἥ τε ἀραιότης καὶ τὸ μέγεθος· μᾶλλον μὲν γὰρ καὶ ἧττον ἀραιὸν καὶ μᾶλλον καὶ ἧττον μέγα τοῖς παραφρονοῦσι γίγνεται τὸ πνεῦμα, μικρὸν δὲ καὶ πυκνὸν οὐδέποτε. τοσαῦτα ἀρκεῖν μοι δοκεῖ περὶ τῶν ἀνωμαλιῶν εἰδέναι τῶν τε κατὰ μίαν ἐνέργειαν καὶ τῶν ἐν πλείοσι συνισταμένων. καὶ γὰρ καὶ περὶ τούτων εἴρηται τὰ εἰκότα τῇ κοινωνίᾳ τοῦ λόγου. καὶ μέντοι σαφῶς μὲν αὗται σύνθετοι, παμπόλλων ἐνεργειῶν εἰς γένεσίν τε καὶ διάγνωσιν δεόμεναι· αἱ δὲ κατὰ μίαν, ὡς καὶ πρόσθεν εἴρηται, τάχ’ ἄν τῳ δόξειαν ὑπάρχειν ἁπλαῖ. οὐ μόνον δὲ περὶ τῶν συστηματικῶν ἀνωμαλιῶν, ἀλλὰ καὶ περὶ τῶν ἀταξιῶν ἐν ταὐτῷ διήλθομεν. ἐν εἴδει γάρ τινι τῶν ἀνωμαλιῶν εἰσιν, ὡς κᾀν τοῖς περὶ σφυγμῶν ἐδείχθη. Ἐπὶ τὰς ἐπιλοίπους οὖν αὖθις ἐπανέλθωμεν δυσπνοίας συνθέτους, καὶ πρώτας γε αὐτῶν προχειρισώμεθα τὰς κατὰ μίαν ἐνέργειαν ἡντιναοῦν συνισταμένας.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τῇ κοινωνίᾳ τοῦ λόγου

Rightly, then, was it said earlier that in all the organs that are moved in respiration, when they are in pain, the breathing that follows is small and frequent; and by the same reasoning that in deliriums it becomes large and rare, even if, most especially, 809 some unevenness attends these too. For in them rarity and largeness predominate; for the breathing of those who are delirious becomes more and less rare, and more and less large, but never small and frequent. So much seems to me sufficient to know about the unevennesses, both those occurring in a single activity and those constituted in several. For indeed about these too what was fitting has been said, owing to the community of the argument. And indeed these latter are clearly composite, requiring very many activities both for their generation and for their diagnosis; whereas those in a single activity, as was also said before, might perhaps seem to someone to be simple. And we have gone through, in the same place, not only the systematic unevennesses but also the disorders. For they belong to a certain species of the unevennesses, as was shown also in the works on pulses. Let us therefore return again to the remaining composite dyspnoeas, and let us take in hand first among them those constituted in any single activity whatsoever.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τῇ κοινωνίᾳ τοῦ λόγου, συστηματικῶν, ἀταξιῶν, ἐν ταὐτῷ

It was therefore rightly said earlier that, when any of the organs moved in respiration is in pain, small and frequent breathing follows; and, by the same reasoning, that in deliria the breathing becomes large and infrequent, even though some irregularity very much 809 attends these too. For in them infrequency and largeness predominate: in those who are delirious the breathing becomes more or less infrequent and more or less large, but never small and frequent. This much seems to me sufficient to know about the irregularities, both those in a single activity and those that arise in several. For about the latter too what was fitting has been said, through what the account has in common. And indeed these latter are clearly compound, since they require a great many activities both for their generation and for their diagnosis; whereas those in a single activity, as was also said before, might perhaps seem to someone to be simple. In the same discussion we went through not only the systematic irregularities but also the instances of disorder. For these are a kind of irregularity, as was shown also in the works on pulses. Let us then return again to the remaining compound difficulties of breathing, and let us first take up those of them that arise in any one single activity.

Greek (Kühn)

ἡ μὲν δὴ μεγάλη τε καὶ ἅμα ταχεῖα εἰσπνοὴ τῆς μὲν χρείας ἐπειγούσης γίνεται, τῶν δ’ ὀργάνων εὐπειθῶν ὑπαρχόντων, τῆς 810 δυνάμεως δὲ ἤτοι τὴν ἰδίαν εὐρωστίαν εὐρωστούσης, ἢ μικρὸν μεταβεβλημένης. ἀποδείξεως δὲ οὐ δεῖ τῷ λόγῳ πρὸς τοὺς μεμνημένους τῶν ἔμπροσθεν. ἡ δ’ αὖ μεγάλη μὲν, βραδεῖα δὲ μόνοις τοῖς παραπαίουσι γίνεται, καὶ τούτοις οὐκ ἀεί. καὶ πρόδηλος τῆς αἰτίας ὁ λογισμὸς κᾀνταῦθα ἐκ τῶν προειρημένων, ὥσπερ καὶ ὅταν μικρὸν εἰσπνέωσι καὶ βραδέως, οἷς ἱκανῶς ἔψυκται τὸ ἔμφυτον θερμόν. ἡ δὲ λοιπὴ καὶ τετάρτη συζυγία μικρότης ἦν ἅμα τῷ τάχει καὶ γίγνεται διὰ παντὸς μὲν ἐπ’ οὐδεμιᾷ διαθέσει ἁπλῇ, καθάπερ οὐδὲ ἡ μεγάλη τε ἅμα καὶ βραδεῖα, ἣν ἔσθ’ ὅτε τοῖς παραπαίουσι συμπίπτειν ἔφαμεν· ἕπεται μέντοι κᾂν εἰ μὴ διηνεκῶς, ἀλλ’ ὡς τὸ πολύ γε, συνελθόντων εἰς ταὐτὸν ἀλγήματός τε καὶ χρείας αὐξήσεως. μικρὸν μὲν γὰρ ἐν τοῖς ἀλγήμασιν ἐξ ἀνάγκης ἀναπνέουσιν, ἀπληρώτου δὲ τῆς χρείας μενούσης, διά τε τὴν αὐτὴν ταύτην μικρότητα καὶ ὅτι πλεονεκτεῖν ὑπέκειτο καθ’ ἑαυτὴν, οὐκ ἀρκεῖ πυκνωθῆναι μόνον τὰς ἐνεργείας, ἀλλ’ ἀναγκάζονται πολλάκις ἐσπασμένως εἰσπνεῖν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐσπασμένως

Now the inspiration that is large and at the same time rapid occurs when the need is pressing, the organs are compliant, and the 810 faculty either enjoys its own proper vigour or has been altered only a little. The argument requires no demonstration for those who remember what was said earlier. The inspiration that is large but slow, on the other hand, occurs only in those who are delirious, and not always even in these. And here too the reasoning about the cause is evident from what has been said before, just as it is also when those in whom the innate heat has been sufficiently cooled breathe in small and slowly. The remaining and fourth conjugation was smallness together with rapidity, and it occurs constantly in no simple condition, just as neither does the large and at the same time slow inspiration, which we said sometimes befalls those who are delirious; it does follow, however, if not continuously, at least for the most part, when pain and an increase of the need come together in the same patient. For in pains they necessarily breathe small; and since the need remains unfulfilled, both because of this very smallness and because it was assumed to be in excess on its own account, it does not suffice for the activities merely to become frequent, but they are often compelled to breathe in in a spasmodic manner.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: διὰ παντὸς μὲν ἐπ’ οὐδεμιᾷ διαθέσει ἁπλῇ, πλεονεκτεῖν ὑπέκειτο καθ’ ἑαυτὴν, ἐσπασμένως

A large and at the same time fast inspiration occurs when the need is pressing, the instruments are compliant, and the 810 faculty either is strong with its own proper strength or has been only slightly altered. The argument needs no demonstration for those who remember what was said earlier. The inspiration that is large but slow, on the other hand, occurs only in those who are deranged, and not always in them. Here too the reasoning about the cause is plain from what was said before, just as it is also when they breathe in small and slowly, in whom the innate heat has been considerably cooled. The remaining, fourth pairing was smallness together with speed. It does not invariably arise upon any simple condition, just as the inspiration that is large and at the same time slow does not either, which we said sometimes befalls those who are deranged. It does, however, follow, if not continually, at least for the most part, when pain and an increase of the need come together in the same person. For in pains they of necessity breathe small. Since the need remains unfulfilled, both because of this very smallness and because it was assumed to be in excess on its own account, it is not enough that the activities merely be made frequent; they are often compelled to breathe in spasmodically.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

And analogously 811 to what has been said, one must also discover the conditions in the case of the conjugations relating to the expirations. For indeed in these cases too we shall find either the faculty or one of the organs affected, or the measure of the heat altered. For the residues which we call, as it were, sooty and fuliginous and smoky within the genus of heat are all of them earthy and, as it were, half-concocted by the innate heat, just as smoke is by fire. For in the case of the latter all the smoky residues of the material plainly appear acrid and hot, showing that they have indeed received into themselves the power of the fire, yet have not been entirely mastered by it; for in that case flame would have come to be, and no longer smoke.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: λιγνυώδη, ὕλης, ἐπὶ τούτου

Analogously 811 to what has been said, one must also discover the conditions underlying the combinations that concern expirations. For in the case of fire it is plainly evident that all the smoky residues of the material are acrid and hot, which indicates that they have received into themselves the power of the fire, yet have not been wholly mastered by it; for in that case flame would have come into being, and no longer smoke.

Greek (Kühn)

ὥστε εἴ τις θερμασίας καπνώδους ἐν τοῖς σώμασι τῶν ζώων πλεονεκτούσης, τὴν ἐκπνοὴν λέγοι μεγάλην τε ἅμα γίνεσθαι καὶ ταχεῖαν, οὐκ ἂν ἁμάρτοι· δῆλον δ’ ὅτι καὶ τὴν δύναμιν ἐῤῥῶσθαι χρὴ τηνικαῦτα, καὶ τῶν ὀργάνων μηδὲν πεπονθέναι· κατὰ ταὐτὰ δὲ καὶ τὴν μεγάλην τε ἅμα καὶ βραδεῖαν ἐνίοις τῶν παραφρονούντων, ὥσπερ εἶχε κᾀπὶ τῆς εἰσπνοῆς· οὕτω δὲ καὶ τὴν μικρὰν ἅμα καὶ βραδεῖαν, οἷς μηδὲν 812 συνήθροιστο καπνῶδες περίττωμα, καὶ τὴν μικρὰν καὶ ταχεῖαν, οἷς ἤθροιστο, καὶ ὀδυνᾶται τῶν μορίων ὅσα δηλονότι κινεῖται κατὰ τὰς ἀναπνοάς. ἀεὶ γὰρ χρὴ τούτων μεμνῆσθαι, κᾂν τῇ λέξει ποτὲ παραλειφθῇ. Αὗται μὲν οὖν αἱ τέτταρες συζυγίαι τῶν δυσπνοιῶν καθ’ ἑκάτερον ἰδίᾳ τῆς ὅλης ἀναπνοῆς μόριον συνίστανται· ἕτεραι δὲ κατὰ τὸν οἷον ῥυθμὸν αὐτῆς, οὐ καθ’ ἑκάτερον, ὅταν δὲ παραβάλλωμεν εἰσπνοὴν ἐκπνοῇ, καθάπερ ἐν τοῖς σφυγμοῖς διαστολὴν συστολῇ, ῥυθμὸν τὸ τοιοῦτον ὀνομάζομεν. δῆλον δ’ ὅτι τὴν αὐτὴν θεωρίαν ὁ τόπος ἐκδέξεται τῇ περὶ τῶν ἐν τοῖς σφυγμοῖς ῥυθμῶν ὑφ’ Ἡροφίλου γεγραμμένῃ. εἴρηται μὲν οὖν ἡμῖν περὶ αὐτῶν ὅσα χρὴ κᾀν ταῖς περὶ τῶν σφυγμῶν πραγματείαις· εἰρήσεται δὲ καὶ νῦν ὅσον οἷόν τε διὰ βραχυτάτων, οἷς χρὴ προσέχοντα τὸν νοῦν οἷον σκοποῖς τισιν ἐξευρίσκειν τὰς αἰτίας ῥᾳδίως τῆς ἐν ταῖς ἀναπνοαῖς ἀῤῥυθμίας.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: οὐ καθ’ ἑκάτερον, ὅταν δὲ

So that if someone were to say that, when smoky heat predominates in the bodies of animals, the expiration becomes both great and at the same time rapid, he would not err; and it is clear that at that time the faculty must also be strong, and none of the organs must be affected. In the same way, too, the expiration that is both great and at the same time slow occurs in some of those who are delirious, just as was the case with inspiration as well; and so too the small and at the same time slow one, in those in whom no 812 smoky residue has accumulated, and the small and rapid one, in those in whom it has accumulated and in whom there is pain in those parts, namely, which are moved in respiration. For one must always bear these things in mind, even if at some point they are omitted in the wording. These four conjugations of dyspnoeas, then, are constituted in each part of the whole respiration taken separately; but others are constituted according to what may be called its rhythm, not in each part [separately]; and when we compare inspiration with expiration, just as in the pulses diastole with systole, we name such a thing rhythm. And it is clear that this topic will admit the same theory as that written by Herophilus concerning the rhythms in the pulses. We have, then, said all that is needed about them also in the treatises on the pulses; but it will be stated now too, as far as possible in the briefest terms, what things one must attend to, as to certain targets, in order to discover easily the causes of arrhythmia in respirations.

So that if someone were to say that, when smoky heat predominates in the bodies of animals, the expiration becomes at once large and fast, he would not be wrong; but it is clear that at that time the faculty must also be strong, and none of the organs must be affected. In the same way he would not be wrong in saying that the expiration which is at once large and slow occurs in some of those who are delirious, just as was the case with inspiration; and likewise the small and at the same time slow expiration in those in whom no smoky residue had 812 accumulated, and the small and fast in those in whom it had accumulated and in whom those of the parts that are, evidently, moved in respiration are in pain. These, then, are the four combinations of the dyspnoeas that are constituted in each part of the whole respiration separately; but there are others according to what is, as it were, its rhythm, not in each part separately: when we compare inspiration with expiration, just as in the pulses diastole with systole, we call such a thing rhythm. Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For the points which we have just made in the preceding discussion concerning each part of the whole respiration 813 severally—I mean inspiration and expiration—these very points I ask that one consider now as well with regard to their arrhythmias. For example—for it is no worse to demonstrate what is being said in some single case by way of illustration—let the inspiration be slower and smaller than the natural one, but the expiration faster and larger: we shall say that in those so disposed the natural heat has been diminished, while the smoky heat has been increased. And again, in the case of another arrhythmia holding in the reverse way, so that the inspiration is large and fast, but the expiration small and slow, we shall say that the natural heat has been increased, but that there is no smoky residue. For one must always conceive of the hot as twofold in kind: the one proper to and connate with the body, the other acquired and spurious. It is clear, then, that such a conjunction alters nothing of what belongs to each of the two taken by itself.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For if the inspiration should happen to become small and slow, it indicates a cooling of the natural heat, however the expiration may be disposed; and if the expiration 814 is disposed in the same way, it will indicate the same thing; for indeed if it should at some time become large and quick, it will show a heat that is in excess and contrary to nature, however the conditions of the inspiration may stand; whence such a combination teaches nothing more for prognoses than what has already been learned about each of the two separately. We must, then, direct our attention only to those composite dyspnoeas in which the simple ones are common to several conditions and for this reason indicate nothing clear, but, when yoked together with certain others, arrive at a secure distinction. For largeness of respiration is, in and of itself, a certain simple dyspnoea; but it is a secure sign of nothing, since it arises both when the need is urgent and when the intellect is damaged; but if it is constituted together with density, it indicates urgency of the need; if with rarity, delirium.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: δυνάμεως

In the same way smallness too, by itself, indicates either that some part is in pain or that the need is slackened, so that it too shows nothing clear; but when it becomes compound, with frequency it indicates pain, with rarity a slackening of the 815 need. And even if you were to take up frequency by itself alone, or rarity, you would find that neither of these shows anything clear without being determined by being combined with some other species of dyspnoea. For frequent together with large indicates an increase of the faculty; frequent together with small, pain of one of the organs that are moved together in respirations; and rare, which follows upon deliriums and chillings, if it be with largeness, indicates the former of those mentioned, if with smallness, the latter. And for this reason, of all the compound dyspnoeas such conjugations are the most useful, indicating clearly and securely one condition each: frequency together with smallness, pain; but if it occurs with largeness, an increase of the need; rarity with largeness, delirium, with smallness, a slackening of the need. These conjugations come to be four, the differences in the activities with respect to largeness and smallness being mixed with the differences of the states of rest.

δύο γὰρ ἀντιθέσεις 816 ἀλλήλαις ἐπαλλαττόμεναι, τέτταρας ποιοῦσι τὰς συζεύξεις. ὥστε κᾀνταῦθα δῆλον ἤδη γέγονεν, ὡς οὔτε πλείους οὔτ’ ἐλάττους ἐνδέχεται τὰς ἐκ τῶν ἀντιθέσεων τούτων συμπλοκὰς γενέσθαι. ἀλλ’ ἐπὶ μὲν ταῖς ἠρεμίαις ἀντίθεσις μία μόνη συμβέβηκεν ἡ κατὰ πυκνότητα καὶ ἀραιότητα, ταῖς δ’ ἐνεργείαις οὐχ ἡ κατὰ μέγεθος καὶ μικρότητα μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ ἡ κατὰ τάχος καὶ βραδυτῆτα. τέτταρες δὲ δηλονότι καὶ κατὰ τὰς τούτων ἐπαλλάξεις γενήσονται αἱ συζυγίαι· πρώτη μὲν πυκνὴ καὶ ταχεῖα; δευτέρα πυκνὴ ἅμα καὶ βραδεῖα, τρίτη ἀραιὰ ἅμα καὶ ταχεῖα, καὶ τετάρτη βραδεῖά τε ἅμα καὶ ἀραιά. γνωρίζουσι δ’ οὐδὲν ἁπλῶς ἐξ ἀνάγκης, ὡς αἱ πρότεραι τέτταρες, ἀλλ’ ὧδε χρὴ διορίζεσθαι· πολλῆς μὲν εἰς πυκνότητα μεταπτώσεως γινομένης, ὀλίγης δὲ τῆς εἰς τάχος, ὀδύνης τε ἅμα καὶ χρείας ἐστὶν αὔξησις· εἰ δ’ ἔμπαλιν εἰς τάχος μὲν πλέον, εἰς πυκνότητα δὲ ἔλαττον ἐξίσταται τοῦ κατὰ φύσιν, τὴν χρείαν μόνην ἐπιτετάσθαι δηλώσει.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For two oppositions, 816 interchanged with one another, produce four conjunctions. So that here too it has already become clear that the combinations arising from these oppositions can be neither more nor fewer. But in the case of the rests there is only one single opposition, that according to density and rarity, whereas in the activities there is not only that according to largeness and smallness, but also that according to speed and slowness. And clearly four conjugations will arise also according to the interchanges of these: the first dense and quick; the second dense and at the same time slow; the third rare and at the same time quick; and the fourth slow and at the same time rare. But they do not indicate anything simply and of necessity, as the former four do; rather, one must make the distinction as follows: when the change toward density is great but that toward speed is small, there is an increase both of pain and, at the same time, of the need; but if, conversely, it departs from the natural state more toward speed and less toward density, it will show that the need alone has been intensified.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ταχεῖα; (the Greek question mark after πρώτη μὲν πυκνὴ καὶ ταχεῖα appears to be a misprint for a raised point; treated as a list separator), συζεύξεις (rendered 'conjunctions', apparently synonymous here with συζυγίαι), χρείας / χρείαν (rendered 'need'; could also be 'use' or 'requirement')

For two antitheses 816, interchanged with one another, produce four conjunctions. So here too it has already become clear that neither more nor fewer combinations can arise from these antitheses. But in the case of the rests there is only one antithesis, that according to frequency and infrequency, whereas in the case of the activities there is not only that according to largeness and smallness, but also that according to speed and slowness. And clearly four pairings will likewise arise from the interchanges of these: first, frequent and fast; second, frequent and at the same time slow; third, infrequent and at the same time fast; and fourth, slow and at the same time infrequent. But these make nothing known simply and of necessity, as the former four do; rather one must make distinctions as follows: when the change towards frequency is great, but that towards speed slight, there is an increase both of pain and of need; but if, conversely, it departs from the natural state more towards speed and less towards frequency, it will show that the need alone has been intensified.

Greek (Kühn)

συνδραμεῖται δὲ τῇ μὲν τοιαύτῃ δυσπνοίᾳ πάντως καὶ μέγεθος, 817 τῇ δὲ ἑτέρᾳ τῇ πρὸ αὐτῆς εἰρημένῃ σμικρότης, ὥστ’ οὐδὲν δεησόμεθα τάχους μνημονεύειν. εἰ γὰρ ἡ ἀδιόριστος μὲν κατὰ τάχος καὶ πυκνότητα συζυγία μηδὲν ἡμῖν δηλοῖ σαφὲς, εἰς διορισμὸν δ’ ἐλθοῦσα, τοῦ μὲν τάχους πλεονεκτοῦντος, εὐθὺς καὶ μετὰ μεγέθους γίγνεται, τῆς δὲ πυκνότητος κρατούσης, ἐξ ἀνάγκης ἑπομένην ἔχει τὴν μικρότητα, πυκνὴ μὲν ἅμα καὶ μεγάλη κατὰ τὸν ἕτερον ἔσται διορισμὸν, πυκνὴ δ’ ἅμα καὶ μικρὰ κατὰ τὸν ἕτερον. οὕτω δὲ καὶ πυκνὴ καὶ βραδεῖα συζυγία δειχθήσεται· πλεονεκτούσης μὲν γὰρ τῆς πυκνότητος, ἄλγημα μόνον ἐνδείξεται· τοσοῦτον δὲ ἐξισταμένη τοῦ κατὰ φύσιν, ὅσον καὶ κατὰ βραδυτῆτα, συνθέτου διαθέσεως ἔσται γνώρισμα. καὶ γὰρ τὴν δύναμιν μεμειῶσθαι δηλώσει καὶ ὀδυνᾶσθαί τι μόριον. οὕτω δὲ καὶ εἰ πλεονεκτεῖ τῆς πυκνότητος ἡ βραδυτὴς, ἔσται γνώρισμα διαθέσεως τῆς ἤδη λελεγμένης, ἀλλ’ ἔλαττον μὲν ὀδυνᾶσθαι δηλώσει τοὺς οὕτως ἔχοντας, μειζόνως δὲ τὴν δύναμιν μεμειῶσθαι. οὔκουν οὐδὲ ταύτης εἰς τὰς προγνώσεις δεόμεθά τι τῆς συζυγίας.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

And with such a dyspnoea largeness will in every case also concur, 817 while with the other one, mentioned before it, smallness, so that we shall have no need to mention speed. For if the undetermined conjunction in respect of speed and frequency indicates nothing clear to us, but, when it comes to a determination, with speed predominating it at once occurs together with largeness, whereas with frequency prevailing it necessarily has smallness following upon it, then it will be frequent and at the same time large according to the one determination, and frequent and at the same time small according to the other. And in the same way the frequent and slow conjunction too will be shown: for when frequency predominates, it will indicate pain alone; but when it departs from the natural state as much as it does also in respect of slowness, it will be a sign of a composite condition. For it will indicate both that the faculty has been diminished and that some part is in pain. And likewise, if slowness predominates over frequency, it will be a sign of the condition already mentioned, but it will indicate that those so disposed are in less pain, while the faculty has been more greatly diminished. Therefore we have no need of this conjunction either for prognoses.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: δύναμιν (rendered 'faculty'; could be 'strength'), δειχθήσεται (rendered 'be explained'; literally 'be shown')

With a difficulty of breathing of this kind, largeness will in every case also concur, 817 and with the other one, mentioned before it, smallness, so that we shall have no need to mention speed. For if the combination of speed and frequency, when left undistinguished, shows us nothing clear, but, once brought to a distinction, comes about straightaway together with largeness when speed predominates, and necessarily has smallness following upon it when frequency prevails, then it will be frequent and at the same time large according to the one distinction, and frequent and at the same time small according to the other. In this way the frequent and slow combination will also be explained. For when frequency predominates, it will indicate pain alone; but when it departs from the natural state as far as it also does in slowness, it will be a sign of a compound condition. For it will show both that the faculty has been diminished and that some part is in pain. So too, if slowness predominates over frequency, it will be a sign of the condition already stated, but it will show that those so affected are in less pain, and that their faculty has been diminished to a greater degree. Accordingly, we have no need of this combination either for prognoses.

Greek (Kühn)

ἐν μὲν γὰρ ταῖς πρώταις ῥηθείσαις τέτταρσιν, ἃς χρησιμωτάτας 818 ἐλέγομεν ὑπάρχειν, ἔχομεν γνωρίσματα καὶ πόνου καὶ χρείας μειώσεως μεγάλα. καὶ ἡ τρίτη δὲ ῥηθεῖσα συζυγία, ἡ ἀραιά τε ἅμα καὶ ταχεῖα, δηλώσει μὲν οὐδὲν ἐξ ἀνάγκης, συνεδρεύει δ’ ἤτοι παραφροσύναις τισὶν, ἢ καὶ σὺν αὐταῖς ταῖς τῆς χρείας αὐξήσεσιν. εἴρηται δὲ καὶ πρόσθεν, ὡς τῶν παραπαιόντων ἀραιὰ μὲν πάντως ἐστὶν ἡ ἀναπνοὴ καὶ μεγάλη, ταχεῖα δὲ οὐ πάντως, ὥσπερ οὐδὲ βραδεῖα· γίγνεσθαι γὰρ ἑκάτερον αὐτῶν ἀτάκτως τε καὶ ἀνωμάλως· ὅταν μέντοι συνέλθῃ καὶ ἡ τῆς χρείας αὔξησις, ἀναγκάζονται τηνικαῦτα καὶ πολὺ μεῖζον ἀναπνεῖν ἢ εἰ παρέπαιον μόνον, καὶ ἤδη τε καὶ θᾶττον, ὥστε καὶ ταύτης τῆς συζυγίας οὐδὲν εἰς τὰς προγνώσεις δεόμεθα, τὰ δι’ αὐτῆς δηλούμενα μετὰ τοῦ διορισμοῦ φθάνοντες ἔχειν ἐν ταῖς πρώταις συζυγίαις τέτταρσι. λοιπὸν δ’ ἦν ἡ τετάρτη τῶν κατὰ τοῦτο τὸ γένος συνθέτων δυσπνοιῶν, ἡ βραδεῖά τε ἅμα καὶ ἀραιὰ, ἥ τις καὶ αὐτὴ παραφροσύναις τέ τισι συνεδρεύει καὶ χρείας μειώσεσιν. οὐκοῦν οὐδὲ ταύτης οὐκέτι προσδεόμεθα· καὶ γὰρ παραφροσύνην καὶ χρείαν μειουμένην ἐγνωρίζομεν ἐκ τῶν πρώτων τεττάρων συζυγιῶν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἤδη τε καὶ θᾶττον

For in the first four that were mentioned, which we said were the most useful, 818 we have great signs both of pain and of diminution of the need. And the third conjugation mentioned, the rare and at the same time rapid, will indicate nothing of necessity, but it accompanies either certain deliriums, or also, together with these, increases of the need. It has also been said before that the respiration of those who are delirious is in every case rare and large, but not in every case rapid, just as it is not in every case slow either; for each of these occurs irregularly and unevenly. When, however, an increase of the need also coincides, then they are compelled both to breathe much more largely than if they were merely delirious, and now also more rapidly, so that we have no need of this conjugation either for prognoses, since we already have what is indicated through it, together with the distinction, in the first four conjugations. There remained the fourth of the compound dyspnoeas in this genus, the slow and at the same time rare, which itself also accompanies certain deliriums and diminutions of the need. Therefore we have no further need of this one either; for we recognised both delirium and diminishing need from the first four conjugations.

For in the first four that were mentioned, which 818 we said were the most useful, we have great signs both of pain and of a diminution of the need. Greek (Kühn)

819 Οὐ μόνον ἐκ τοῦ δεῖξαι τὴν κατὰ τάχος τε καὶ βραδυτῆτα διαφορὰν οὐκ ἀναγκαίαν εἰς τὰς προγνώσεις, οὔτ’ εἰ μόνην οὔτ’ εἰ συμπλέκοις ταῖς ἄλλαις, τὸ χρήσιμον ἐκείνων γίνεται εὔδηλον, ἀλλὰ κᾀξ αὐτῆς τῆς φύσεως τοῦ πράγματος φαίνεται. ἐπεὶ γὰρ τρία τὰ πρῶτα γένη τῶν αἰτίων ἐστὶ τῆς ἀναπνοῆς, ἡ χρεία καὶ ἡ δύναμις καὶ τὰ ὄργανα, δῆλον ὡς, ἂν εὕρωμεν ἐκ τίνων καὶ πόσων δυσπνοιῶν τὰς ἔν αὐτοῖς διαθέσεις γνωριοῦμεν, αὗται μὲν ἡμῖν χρησιμώταται πρὸς τὰς προγνώσεις ἔσονται, τὰς δ’ ἄλλας ἁπάσας ἐκ περιουσίας ἐπισκεψόμεθα. ἀλλ’ εὑρέθησαν αὔξησιν μὲν τῆς χρείας ἡ μεγάλη καὶ πυκνὴ, μείωσιν δὲ ἡ ἐναντία, ἡ μικρὰ καὶ ἀραιὰ, δηλοῦσαι, παραφροσύνης τε ἴδιος ἡ μεγάλη τε καὶ ἀραιὰ, πόνου δ’ ἡ μικρὰ καὶ πυκνή. προσίεσαν δ’ ἔξωθεν αἱ ἀνώμαλοι, ποτὲ μὲν τοῦ μυώδους γένους τὰς διαθέσεις, ποτὲ δὲ καὶ τῶν τραχειῶν ἀρτηριῶν τὰς στενοχωρίας δηλοῦσαι.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐκ περιουσίας, προσίεσαν

819 Not only from showing that the difference in respect of speed and slowness is not necessary for prognoses, neither if you take it alone nor if you combine it with the others, does the usefulness of those become quite evident, but it is apparent also from the very nature of the matter. For since the primary kinds of the causes of respiration are three—the need, the faculty, and the organs—it is clear that, if we discover from which and from how many dyspnoeas we shall recognise the conditions in these, then these will be the most useful to us for prognoses, while all the others we shall examine as something over and above. But it was found that the large and frequent respiration indicates increase of the need, and its opposite, the small and rare, indicates diminution; and that the large and rare is peculiar to delirium, the small and frequent to pain. And the uneven ones came in from outside, indicating sometimes the conditions of the muscular kind, and sometimes also the narrowings of the rough arteries.
